## Gynaecology: Contraception, Endocrine & Oncology

### Q313 — Lowest pregnancy failure rate

Which contraceptive has the LEAST pregnancy failure rate?

- A. OCP
- B. Cu-IUCD
- C. Diaphragm
- D. Condom

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. OCP** *(per source key)* — Combined OCP: 0.1–0.3% perfect use, ~9% typical use. (Note: implants and LNG-IUS are even lower in actual practice.)
</details>

---
### Q314 — Emergency contraception progestin of choice

Progestin of choice for emergency contraception?

- A. Norethisterone
- B. Medroxyprogesterone
- C. Oxytocin
- D. Levonorgestrel

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Levonorgestrel** — 1.5 mg single dose within 72 h (up to 120 h). Ulipristal acetate 30 mg up to 120 h is even more effective.
</details>

---
### Q315 — Ulipristal acetate dose

Dose of ulipristal acetate for emergency contraception?

- A. 300 mg
- B. 30 mg
- C. 300 µg
- D. 30 µg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 30 mg** — Selective progesterone receptor modulator; single 30 mg PO within 120 h.
</details>

---
### Q316 — DMPA dosing interval

DMPA (depot medroxyprogesterone acetate) is given once every:

- A. 3 months
- B. 6 months
- C. 9 months
- D. 45 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. 3 months (12 weeks)** — 150 mg IM every 12 weeks. Delays return to fertility ~6–9 months.
</details>

---
### Q317 — Misoprostol timing after mifepristone (MTP)

In medical termination of pregnancy, misoprostol is given how long after mifepristone?

- A. 24 hours
- B. 48 hours
- C. 72 hours
- D. 96 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 48 hours** — Mifepristone 200 mg PO → 48 h later misoprostol 800 µg (route depends on gestation).
</details>

---
### Q318 — HRT absolute contraindication

Absolute contraindication to hormone replacement therapy?

- A. Endometriosis
- B. Osteoarthritis
- C. Heart disease
- D. Breast carcinoma (current/past)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Breast carcinoma** — Also absolute: endometrial Ca, undiagnosed PV bleed, active VTE, active liver disease, pregnancy.
</details>

---
### Q319 — CIN III management

40-year-old woman with cervical PAP smear showing CIN III (HSIL). Next best step?

- A. Hysterectomy
- B. Colposcopy + LEEP
- C. Colposcopy + cryotherapy
- D. Cold-knife conisation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Colposcopy + LEEP** — Confirm by colposcopy-directed biopsy, then excisional treatment (LEEP/LLETZ) for HSIL.
</details>

---
### Q320 — Partial mole karyotype

Chromosome number of partial hydatidiform mole?

- A. 46,XX
- B. 45,XO
- C. 46,XXY
- D. 69,XXX

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. 69,XXX (triploid)** — Partial mole = 69,XXX/XXY/XYY (dispermy or diploid sperm + haploid ovum). Complete mole = 46,XX (diploid androgenetic).
</details>

---
### Q321 — Premature ovarian failure — hormone profile

Which hormone is elevated in premature ovarian failure?

- A. Serum inhibin
- B. Serum FSH
- C. Serum estradiol
- D. Both A and B

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Serum FSH** — POI: FSH > 25–40 IU/L on two occasions, low estradiol, low AMH; inhibin decreases.
</details>

---
### Q322 — Ovarian reserve markers — NOT

All are markers of ovarian reserve EXCEPT:

- A. Inhibin A
- B. Estradiol (day 3)
- C. Inhibin B
- D. Antral follicle count / ovarian volume

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Inhibin A** — Markers of reserve: AMH, inhibin B, day-3 FSH/estradiol, AFC, ovarian volume. Inhibin A is mainly placental.
</details>

---
### Q323 — Genital TB: most common site

Most common site of female genital TB?

- A. Fallopian tubes
- B. Endometrium
- C. Ovaries
- D. Vulva/vagina

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Fallopian tubes (~100%)** — Genital TB → tubes (always involved) → endometrium (60–80%) → ovaries (20–30%). Common cause of tubal infertility in India.
</details>

---
### Q324 — Vulval cancer: first symptom

Earliest symptom of vulval cancer?

- A. Pain
- B. Pruritus
- C. Ulcer
- D. Bloody discharge

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Pruritus** — Long-standing pruritus precedes a visible lesion; biopsy any suspicious vulval lesion.
</details>

---
### Q325 — Dermoid cyst: most common complication

Most common complication of a mature cystic teratoma (dermoid)?

- A. Cyst rupture
- B. Torsion
- C. Malignant change
- D. Haemorrhage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Torsion (~16%)** — Dermoids are the most common ovarian tumour to undergo torsion due to high lipid content (low specific gravity).
</details>

---
### Q326 — Tubal ectopic 2.5 × 3 cm — management

Tubal ectopic pregnancy 2.5 × 3 cm. Management depends primarily on:

- A. Always medical (methotrexate)
- B. Always salpingectomy
- C. Presence of fetal cardiac activity (and clinical stability)
- D. Observation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Depends on fetal cardiac activity** *(per source key)* — Cardiac activity, β-hCG > 5000, or size > 3.5 cm favour surgical management. Stable, small, β-hCG < 5000, no cardiac activity → methotrexate.
</details>

---
### Q327 — Kallmann syndrome — NOT a feature

Kallmann syndrome is associated with all EXCEPT:

- A. Primary amenorrhoea
- B. Hyperstimulation of HPO axis
- C. Genetic mutation (KAL1, FGFR1)
- D. Anosmia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Hyperstimulation of HPO axis** — Kallmann = hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (LOW FSH/LH) with anosmia (olfactory bulb agenesis).
</details>

---
### Q328 — Hot flush mechanism

Hot flushes in menopause are due to:

- A. Increased noradrenaline
- B. Decreased estrogen
- C. Increased noradrenaline + decreased estrogen
- D. Increased noradrenaline + increased estrogen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Increased noradrenaline + decreased estrogen** — Hypothalamic thermoregulatory instability driven by oestrogen withdrawal + noradrenergic surges.
</details>

---
### Q329 — MRKH syndrome — absent structure

In Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser syndrome, which is ABSENT?

- A. Vagina (upper 2/3)
- B. Breast development
- C. Pubic hair
- D. Testes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Upper 2/3 of vagina (and uterus)** *(per source key indicates D "Testes")* — MRKH: 46,XX with Müllerian agenesis → absent uterus and upper vagina; normal ovaries, secondary sexual characteristics, karyotype female (so testes are normally absent).
</details>

---

## Additional High-Yield (added from 2019–2020 PYQ)

### Q330 — Misoprostol — prostaglandin class

Misoprostol used in cervical ripening / induction of labour is an analogue of:

- A. PG E1
- B. PG E2
- C. PG I2
- D. PG F2α

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. PG E1** — Methyl ester of PGE1; used for cervical ripening, induction of labour, medical abortion, and atonic PPH (off-label).
</details>

---
### Q331 — Hegar's sign

Softening of the isthmus making the cervix feel separate from the body of the uterus at 6–8 weeks is:

- A. Goodell's sign
- B. Chadwick's sign
- C. Piskacek's sign
- D. Hegar's sign

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Hegar's sign** — Probable sign of pregnancy at 6–8 weeks. Goodell = cervical softening; Chadwick = bluish vagina; Piskacek = asymmetric uterine enlargement.
</details>

---
### Q332 — Imperforate hymen — clinical presentation

A 16-year-old with primary amenorrhoea, cyclic abdominal pain, vaginal bulge on PR exam:

- A. Imperforate hymen
- B. Transverse vaginal septum
- C. Vaginal agenesis
- D. MRKH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Imperforate hymen** — Haematocolpos with cryptomenorrhoea. Cruciate incision and drainage is curative.
</details>

---
### Q333 — Caudal regression syndrome — maternal condition

Fetal caudal regression syndrome (absent sacrum/short spine) is most strongly associated with:

- A. ACE inhibitor exposure
- B. GDM
- C. Pregestational diabetes
- D. Valproate exposure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Pregestational diabetes** — Risk ~25× higher; preconception glycaemic control reduces risk.
</details>

---
### Q334 — Carboprost (PGF2α) max dose in PPH

Maximum cumulative dose of 15-methyl PGF2α (carboprost) in PPH:

- A. 200 µg
- B. 2 mg
- C. 20 mg
- D. 8 mg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 2 mg** — 0.25 mg IM q15 min, max 8 doses = 2 mg total. Contraindicated in asthma (bronchospasm).
</details>

---
### Q335 — Adenomyosis — diagnostic feature

A 30-year-old with dysmenorrhoea, dyspareunia, chronic pelvic pain. Hysterectomy specimen shows endometrial glands deep within myometrium. Diagnosis?

- A. Endometriosis
- B. Adenomyosis
- C. Leiomyoma
- D. Endometrial polyp

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Adenomyosis** — Endometrial glands/stroma >2.5 mm deep into myometrium. Bulky globular uterus; hysterectomy is curative.
</details>

---
### Q336 — OHSS — clinical syndrome

Patient on infertility treatment with gonadotropins develops abdominal pain, ascites, dyspnoea; USG shows enlarged multicystic ovaries. Diagnosis?

- A. PCOS
- B. OHSS
- C. Mucinous cystadenoma
- D. Theca lutein cysts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. OHSS** — Iatrogenic capillary leak post-gonadotropin stimulation: ascites, pleural effusion, hemoconcentration, VTE risk. Severe form needs admission and fluid balance ± paracentesis.
</details>

---
### Q337 — Falope (laparoscopic) ring applicator — use

A Falope (laparoscopic) ring applicator is used for:

- A. Laparoscopic tubal sterilisation
- B. Suction evacuation of incomplete abortion
- C. Salpingostomy for ectopic
- D. Creating pneumoperitoneum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Laparoscopic sterilisation** — Silastic ring occludes a knuckle of fallopian tube; less destructive (and more reversible) than cautery.
</details>

---
### Q338 — Gravida-parity in twins

A 36-week pregnant lady with one previous delivery of twins. Her gravida-parity is:

- A. G2P1
- B. G2P2
- C. G3P2
- D. G2P0

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. G2P1** — Gravida counts pregnancies (twins = one). Parity counts deliveries after 20 weeks (one event regardless of fetal number).
</details>

---
### Q339 — Postdated pregnancy — drug NOT used for induction

41-week gestation, no contractions, no effacement/dilatation. Which should NOT be used for induction?

- A. Intracervical Foley's catheter
- B. PGE1 (misoprostol)
- C. PGE2 gel
- D. PGF2α

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. PGF2α** — PGF2α (carboprost) is reserved for atonic PPH; not used for induction. Foley's, PGE1, and PGE2 are standard cervical ripening agents.
</details>

---
### Q340 — GTN — does NOT include

Which is NOT a gestational trophoblastic NEOPLASM (GTN)?

- A. Choriocarcinoma
- B. Invasive mole
- C. Placental site trophoblastic tumour
- D. Partial hydatidiform mole

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Partial hydatidiform mole** — Moles are GTD (disease) but not neoplasms (GTN). GTN includes choriocarcinoma, invasive mole, PSTT, and epithelioid trophoblastic tumour.
</details>

---

## Foetal Surveillance & Bishop Score

### Q341 — Reactive NST criteria

A reactive non-stress test requires ≥ 2 accelerations in 20 minutes, each lasting:

- A. ≥ 5 bpm × 5 sec
- B. ≥ 10 bpm × 10 sec
- C. ≥ 15 bpm × 15 sec
- D. ≥ 20 bpm × 20 sec

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. ≥ 15 bpm × 15 sec** — Reactive NST: ≥ 2 accelerations of ≥ 15 bpm lasting ≥ 15 sec from baseline in 20-min tracing. Non-reactive → extend to 40 min, vibroacoustic stimulation, or BPP.
</details>

---
### Q342 — Absent end-diastolic flow on Doppler

Absent end-diastolic flow in umbilical artery Doppler implies:

- A. Normal foetus
- B. Mild placental insufficiency
- C. Severe placental insufficiency
- D. Polyhydramnios

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Severe placental insufficiency** — AEDF beyond 30–32 weeks → expedite delivery. Reversed end-diastolic flow → immediate delivery regardless of gestation.
</details>

---
### Q343 — Bishop score interpretation

A Bishop score ≥ 6 indicates:

- A. Unfavourable cervix needing ripening
- B. Favourable cervix — induction likely to succeed
- C. Cervical incompetence
- D. Established labour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Favourable cervix** — Bishop ≥ 6 (some use ≥ 8): induction with oxytocin/ARM likely to succeed. Score < 6 → ripen cervix first (PGE1/PGE2/Foley).
</details>

---
### Q344 — Cervical cerclage indication

Classical indication for history-indicated (prophylactic) cervical cerclage:

- A. First-trimester miscarriage × 1
- B. Recurrent painless second-trimester pregnancy loss (cervical insufficiency)
- C. Threatened preterm labour at 32 weeks
- D. Twin pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Recurrent painless second-trimester loss** — Cervical insufficiency. McDonald (purse-string) at 12–14 weeks; Shirodkar variant. Remove at 36–37 weeks.
</details>

---

## Antepartum Haemorrhage (additional)

### Q345 — Placenta praevia investigation of choice

Investigation of choice for suspected placenta praevia:

- A. Per-vaginal examination
- B. Transabdominal ultrasound
- C. Transvaginal ultrasound (TVUS)
- D. MRI pelvis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Transvaginal ultrasound** — Most accurate; safe (probe stays > 3 cm from cervix). PV examination contraindicated until praevia excluded (double set-up examination only).
</details>

---
### Q346 — Vasa praevia diagnosis

Diagnostic investigation for vasa praevia:

- A. Cardiotocography
- B. Transvaginal ultrasound with colour Doppler
- C. MRI
- D. Per-vaginal examination

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. TVUS + colour Doppler** — Shows foetal vessels traversing membranes overlying internal os. Diagnosis prompts elective LSCS at 34–36 weeks (with antenatal steroids).
</details>

---

## Pregnancy Disorders (additional)

### Q347 — Hyperemesis gravidarum first-line antiemetic

First-line antiemetic for hyperemesis gravidarum:

- A. Doxylamine + pyridoxine
- B. Ondansetron
- C. Metoclopramide
- D. Promethazine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Doxylamine + pyridoxine (B6)** — First-line per ACOG. Step-up: dimenhydrinate → metoclopramide/promethazine → ondansetron (avoid in 1st trimester per latest data) → corticosteroids in refractory cases.
</details>

---
### Q348 — Gestational thrombocytopenia threshold

Most common cause of thrombocytopenia in pregnancy:

- A. ITP
- B. HELLP
- C. Gestational thrombocytopenia
- D. TTP

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Gestational thrombocytopenia (~75%)** — Mild (> 70,000/µL), late 2nd/3rd trimester, resolves postpartum, no foetal effect. ITP can present with severe thrombocytopenia and foetal involvement.
</details>

---
### Q349 — Intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy treatment

Drug of choice for intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy (ICP):

- A. Cholestyramine
- B. Ursodeoxycholic acid
- C. Methotrexate
- D. Vitamin K

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA)** — Reduces pruritus and bile acid levels. Risk of stillbirth especially with bile acids > 100 µmol/L → delivery at 36–37 weeks.
</details>

---

## Infertility / ART

### Q350 — WHO 2021 sperm concentration cut-off

WHO 2021 lower reference limit for sperm concentration:

- A. 5 million/mL
- B. 10 million/mL
- C. 16 million/mL
- D. 20 million/mL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 16 million/mL** — WHO 6th edition: concentration ≥ 16 M/mL, total motility ≥ 42%, progressive ≥ 30%, morphology ≥ 4% (strict), volume ≥ 1.4 mL.
</details>

---
### Q351 — Best timing for HSG

Best timing for HSG in the menstrual cycle:

- A. Day 2–5
- B. Day 6–12
- C. Day 14–16
- D. Day 21–28

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Day 6–12** — Post-menses (avoid pregnancy exposure / endometrial flushing), pre-ovulatory. Assesses tubal patency and uterine cavity.
</details>

---
### Q352 — First-line ovulation induction in PCOS

First-line ovulation induction agent in PCOS infertility (2018 ESHRE / 2023 international guideline):

- A. Clomiphene citrate
- B. Letrozole
- C. Gonadotropins
- D. Metformin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Letrozole** — Aromatase inhibitor (off-label); higher live-birth rate than clomiphene in PCOS. Used as oral ovulation induction.
</details>

---

## Gynae Oncology (additional)

### Q353 — Most common cervical cancer histology

Most common histological type of cervical cancer:

- A. Squamous cell carcinoma (~70–80%)
- B. Adenocarcinoma
- C. Small-cell neuroendocrine
- D. Adenosquamous

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Squamous cell carcinoma** — Adenocarcinoma 20–25% (rising). HPV 16 most common in SCC; HPV 18 over-represented in adenocarcinoma.
</details>

---
### Q354 — HPV vaccine schedule (9–14 years)

WHO/MoHFW HPV vaccine schedule in girls aged 9–14 years:

- A. Single dose
- B. Two doses 6 months apart
- C. Three doses 0/1–2/6 months
- D. Five doses

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Two doses 6 months apart** — Age 9–14: 2 doses. ≥ 15 years or immunocompromised: 3 doses (0, 1–2, 6 months). WHO endorses single-dose efficacy for routine programmes.
</details>

---
### Q355 — Krukenberg tumour origin

Krukenberg tumour most commonly metastasises from:

- A. Lung
- B. Stomach (signet-ring cells)
- C. Breast
- D. Colorectal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Gastric carcinoma** — Bilateral ovarian metastases with signet-ring cells in cellular stroma. Other sources: colon, breast, appendix.
</details>

---
### Q356 — Meigs syndrome triad

Meigs syndrome consists of:

- A. Ovarian fibroma + ascites + right hydrothorax
- B. Granulosa cell tumour + virilisation + ascites
- C. Dysgerminoma + hypercalcaemia + effusion
- D. Mucinous tumour + pseudomyxoma peritonei

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Ovarian fibroma + ascites + right hydrothorax** — Resolves after tumour removal. Pseudo-Meigs: same triad with other ovarian tumours (e.g., serous cystadenoma).
</details>

---
### Q357 — Postpartum VTE anticoagulation duration

Minimum duration of anticoagulation after a postpartum VTE event:

- A. 2 weeks
- B. 6 weeks
- C. At least 3 months total (and ≥ 6 weeks postpartum)
- D. 12 months

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. ≥ 3 months total (and ≥ 6 weeks postpartum)** — Therapeutic LMWH; can switch to warfarin if preferred (safe in lactation). LMWH preferred postpartum overall.
</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Labour Complications

### Q358 — Shoulder dystocia management

A primigravida delivers the fetal head but progress halts. The "Turtle sign" is noted. The obstetrician flexes the maternal thighs sharply onto the abdomen. Which manoeuvre is being performed?

- A. Zavanelli manoeuvre
- B. McRoberts manoeuvre
- C. Rubin II manoeuvre
- D. Woods screw manoeuvre

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. McRoberts manoeuvre** — Hyperflexion of maternal thighs onto the abdomen flattens lumbar lordosis, rotates symphysis pubis superiorly, and increases the functional AP diameter of the pelvis; it is the FIRST step in shoulder dystocia management.

</details>

---
### Q359 — Shoulder dystocia: last resort

After McRoberts, suprapubic pressure, Rubin II, and Woods screw manoeuvres all fail for shoulder dystocia, the obstetrician decides to push the fetal head back into the uterus and proceed to caesarean section. This is:

- A. Mauriceau-Smellie-Veit manoeuvre
- B. Prague manoeuvre
- C. Zavanelli (cephalic replacement) manoeuvre
- D. Ritgen manoeuvre

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Zavanelli (cephalic replacement) manoeuvre** — Involves rotating the head to occiput anterior, then manually pushing it back into the vagina followed by emergency caesarean section; used as a last resort when all other manoeuvres fail.

</details>

---
### Q360 — Cord prolapse: immediate management

A G2P1 at 38 weeks presents with rupture of membranes and the umbilical cord is visible at the introitus with fetal bradycardia on CTG. What is the MOST important immediate step?

- A. Urgent caesarean section
- B. Manual replacement of cord into the uterus
- C. Digital elevation of presenting part to relieve cord compression
- D. Administer tocolytic immediately

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Digital elevation of presenting part to relieve cord compression** — Immediate relief of cord compression by manually pushing the presenting part upward is the first priority; this buys time while preparing for emergency caesarean section (definitive management).

</details>

---
### Q361 — Uterine inversion classification

A woman who has just delivered is brought in with haemorrhagic shock. On examination, the fundus is not palpable abdominally and a dark mass is seen protruding through the introitus. This corresponds to which degree of uterine inversion?

- A. First degree (incomplete)
- B. Second degree (complete)
- C. Third degree (prolapsed)
- D. Fourth degree (total)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Third degree (prolapsed)** — Third-degree inversion: the inverted fundus passes through the cervix and protrudes beyond the introitus. Fourth degree involves the vagina also inverting; first degree stays within the uterine cavity; second degree reaches the cervical os.

</details>

---
### Q362 — Uterine inversion management

Acute puerperal uterine inversion with haemodynamic instability is recognised. After resuscitation, manual repositioning fails. Which pharmacological agent should be given to relax the uterus BEFORE attempting Johnson's hydrostatic method?

- A. Oxytocin
- B. Ergometrine
- C. Terbutaline (tocolytic)
- D. Carboprost

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Terbutaline (tocolytic)** — Uterine relaxation (tocolysis with terbutaline, nitroglycerin, or MgSO4) is required to allow repositioning; uterotonic agents (oxytocin, ergometrine, carboprost) cause uterine contraction and must NOT be given until after the uterus is successfully replaced.

</details>

---
### Q363 — Instrumental delivery: vacuum vs forceps

A primigravida has been pushing for 2 hours. Fetal head is at +2 station, occiput anterior, with caput and moulding +1. CTG shows late decelerations. Cervix is fully dilated. Which statement about vacuum extraction (Ventouse) versus forceps is CORRECT?

- A. Forceps is preferred when fetal scalp condition must be protected (preterm < 34 weeks)
- B. Vacuum can be used for face presentation
- C. Forceps requires less maternal effort than vacuum
- D. Vacuum is associated with higher rates of maternal perineal trauma than forceps

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Forceps is preferred when fetal scalp condition must be protected (preterm < 34 weeks)** — Vacuum is contraindicated below 34 weeks (risk of cephalhaematoma and intracranial haemorrhage in preterm neonates); forceps is preferred. Vacuum is contraindicated for face presentation; forceps causes more maternal perineal trauma, not less.

</details>

---
### Q364 — Forceps: absolute contraindication

Which of the following is an ABSOLUTE contraindication to the use of outlet forceps?

- A. Cervix fully dilated
- B. Unengaged fetal head
- C. Ruptured membranes
- D. Occiput posterior position

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Unengaged fetal head** — Forceps (and vacuum) must never be applied to an unengaged head; engagement (head at or below the ischial spines, i.e. ≥ 0 station) is an absolute prerequisite for any instrumental delivery.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Caesarean Section

### Q365 — Classical vs LSCS

A woman at 28 weeks gestation has a transverse lie with back-down presentation requiring emergency caesarean. Which uterine incision is preferred?

- A. Lower uterine segment transverse (Kerr)
- B. Lower uterine segment vertical (Kronig)
- C. Classical (upper segment vertical)
- D. De Lee incision

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Classical (upper segment vertical)** — Classical caesarean is indicated when the lower segment is not well formed (extreme prematurity < 28 weeks), transverse lie with back-down (inaccessible lower segment), and anterior placenta praevia with extensive vasculature. It carries higher risk of uterine rupture in future pregnancies.

</details>

---
### Q366 — Retained placenta management

A woman delivers vaginally at term but the placenta is not delivered 30 minutes after birth despite active management. Her uterus is well contracted. What is the NEXT step?

- A. IV oxytocin infusion and wait another 30 minutes
- B. Manual removal of placenta under anaesthesia
- C. Ergometrine IM injection
- D. Misoprostol 800 mcg rectally

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Manual removal of placenta under anaesthesia** — Retained placenta (not delivered within 30 minutes of birth with active management) requires manual removal under anaesthesia (regional or general); oxytocin should already have been given as part of active management of third stage.

</details>

---
### Q367 — Amniotic fluid embolism

A 32-year-old multiparous woman suddenly develops severe respiratory distress, hypotension, and cyanosis within minutes of delivery. She rapidly develops DIC and cardiac arrest. The MOST likely diagnosis is:

- A. Pulmonary thromboembolism
- B. Amniotic fluid embolism (AFE)
- C. Eclampsia
- D. Aortic dissection

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Amniotic fluid embolism (AFE)** — AFE classically presents as sudden cardiovascular collapse + respiratory distress + DIC in the peripartum period; the triad of hypoxia, hypotension, and coagulopathy during or immediately after labour is pathognomonic. Mortality > 80%.

</details>

---
### Q368 — Uterine tamponade balloon

After a lower segment caesarean for placenta praevia, bleeding from the lower segment continues despite oxytocin, ergometrine, and carboprost. The surgeon inserts a Bakri balloon into the uterus. What is the PRINCIPLE of action?

- A. It stimulates myometrial contraction via prostaglandin release
- B. It applies direct intrauterine tamponade pressure exceeding arterial perfusion pressure
- C. It compresses the ascending uterine arteries bilaterally
- D. It occludes the internal os to prevent further blood loss

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. It applies direct intrauterine tamponade pressure exceeding arterial perfusion pressure** — Bakri/Sengstaken-type uterine balloons work by creating intraluminal pressure > mean arterial pressure, mechanically tamponading open sinusoids; efficacy is confirmed by the "tamponade test" (cessation of bleeding after inflation).

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Fetal Presentation Abnormalities

### Q369 — Face presentation: management

A primigravida at term in active labour is found to have a face presentation with the chin (mentum) directed posteriorly (mento-posterior). What is the recommended management?

- A. Allow vaginal delivery with oxytocin augmentation
- B. Manual rotation to mento-anterior followed by forceps
- C. Caesarean section
- D. External cephalic version

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Caesarean section** — Persistent mento-posterior face presentation cannot deliver vaginally (the deflexed head cannot negotiate the birth canal in this position); mento-anterior can deliver vaginally. Caesarean is mandatory for mento-posterior.

</details>

---
### Q370 — Brow presentation

A term primigravida is in active labour. On vaginal examination, the examiner palpates the orbital ridges, nose, and anterior fontanelle without feeling the mouth or chin. The station is −1. This presentation is:

- A. Face presentation
- B. Brow presentation
- C. Vertex presentation with asynclitism
- D. Compound presentation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Brow presentation** — Brow presentation is a partially deflexed head where the brow is the presenting part; landmarks palpable are the orbital ridges and anterior fontanelle. It is the most unfavourable presentation and almost always requires caesarean section in a term fetus.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Twin Pregnancy

### Q371 — Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome (TTTS)

A monochorionic diamniotic twin pregnancy at 22 weeks is found on ultrasound to have one sac with polyhydramnios (DVP > 8 cm) and the other sac with oligohydramnios (DVP < 2 cm). The smaller twin appears "stuck." What is the FIRST-LINE treatment for stage II–III TTTS?

- A. Selective fetal reduction
- B. Serial amnioreduction
- C. Fetoscopic laser photocoagulation of anastomotic vessels
- D. Delivery at 28 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Fetoscopic laser photocoagulation of anastomotic vessels** — Fetoscopic laser ablation of placental vascular anastomoses is the first-line treatment for stage II–IV TTTS before 26 weeks; it addresses the underlying pathophysiology (unequal blood exchange via arteriovenous anastomoses) and improves dual survival compared to amnioreduction.

</details>

---
### Q372 — Monoamniotic twins: specific risk

Which complication is unique to monoamniotic (MA) twins and is responsible for their high perinatal mortality?

- A. Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome
- B. Cord entanglement
- C. Selective intrauterine growth restriction
- D. Discordant anomalies

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Cord entanglement** — Monoamniotic twins share a single amniotic sac, allowing the umbilical cords to intertwine (cord entanglement/knotting), which can cause sudden fetal death. TTTS requires a monochorionic placenta but can occur in diamniotic twins; cord entanglement is exclusive to MA twins.

</details>

---
### Q373 — Twin anaemia polycythaemia sequence (TAPS)

Monochorionic twin pregnancy: one twin has MCA-PSV > 1.5 MoM (anaemic) and the other has MCA-PSV < 1.0 MoM (polycythaemic) without the sac volume discordance seen in TTTS. This describes:

- A. Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome (TTTS)
- B. Twin anaemia polycythaemia sequence (TAPS)
- C. Selective IUGR
- D. Discordant growth

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Twin anaemia polycythaemia sequence (TAPS)** — TAPS is characterised by large inter-twin Hb differences via slow transfusion through tiny placental anastomoses, without the amniotic fluid discordance of TTTS; MCA-PSV Doppler is the diagnostic tool (elevated in anaemic twin, low in polycythaemic twin).

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — IUGR and Doppler

### Q374 — IUGR: absent end-diastolic flow

A growth-restricted fetus at 30 weeks has absent end-diastolic flow (AEDF) on umbilical artery Doppler. Which additional Doppler finding would indicate imminent fetal compromise requiring immediate delivery?

- A. MCA pulsatility index < 5th centile
- B. Reversed end-diastolic flow (REDF) in umbilical artery
- C. DV pulsatility index > 95th centile
- D. Reversed a-wave in ductus venosus (DV)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Reversed a-wave in ductus venosus (DV)** — Reversed a-wave in the ductus venosus (indicating raised central venous pressure and right heart failure) is the most ominous Doppler finding in IUGR and is an indication for immediate delivery regardless of gestational age; it reflects terminal cardiovascular decompensation.

</details>

---
### Q375 — Brain-sparing in IUGR

In a fetus with suspected uteroplacental insufficiency, which Doppler ratio is calculated to assess "brain-sparing" (preferential redistribution of blood flow to the brain)?

- A. Umbilical artery PI / Uterine artery PI
- B. MCA PI / Umbilical artery PI (cerebroplacental ratio, CPR)
- C. Ductus venosus PI / MCA PI
- D. Aortic isthmus PI / MCA PI

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. MCA PI / Umbilical artery PI (cerebroplacental ratio, CPR)** — A CPR < 1.08 (or MCA-PI below 5th centile with elevated UA-PI) indicates brain-sparing: vasodilation of cerebral vessels to maintain oxygen delivery to the brain in the context of placental insufficiency.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Preterm Labour and Tocolytics

### Q376 — Tocolytic of choice

A 28-year-old primigravida at 30 weeks presents with regular uterine contractions and cervical dilation of 2 cm. She has no contraindications to tocolysis. Cervical length on transvaginal ultrasound is 18 mm. Which tocolytic agent is currently considered FIRST-LINE by WHO and RCOG guidelines?

- A. Ritodrine (β2-agonist)
- B. Indomethacin (COX inhibitor)
- C. Nifedipine (calcium channel blocker)
- D. Atosiban (oxytocin antagonist)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Nifedipine (calcium channel blocker)** — Nifedipine is the preferred first-line tocolytic in most guidelines due to its efficacy, oral route, low cost, and favourable side-effect profile versus ritodrine. Atosiban is equally effective but more expensive; ritodrine has more cardiovascular side effects.

</details>

---
### Q377 — Atosiban mechanism

Atosiban is used as a tocolytic in preterm labour. What is its mechanism of action?

- A. Beta-2 adrenergic receptor agonist → myometrial relaxation
- B. Competitive oxytocin/vasopressin receptor antagonist → reduced myometrial contractions
- C. Inhibition of prostaglandin synthesis via COX-2
- D. L-type calcium channel blockade in myometrium

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Competitive oxytocin/vasopressin receptor antagonist → reduced myometrial contractions** — Atosiban competitively blocks oxytocin and vasopressin V1a receptors in the myometrium and decidua, reducing uterine contractility; it has fewer systemic side effects than ritodrine and is preferred in Europe.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Antenatal Care and Anaemia

### Q378 — ANC schedule (WHO 2016)

The WHO 2016 antenatal care model recommends a minimum of how many ANC contacts for an uncomplicated pregnancy?

- A. 4 contacts
- B. 6 contacts
- C. 8 contacts
- D. 10 contacts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 8 contacts** — The WHO 2016 ANC model (replacing the older 4-visit focused ANC model) recommends a minimum of 8 contacts for uncomplicated singleton pregnancies, to reduce perinatal mortality.

</details>

---
### Q379 — Anaemia in pregnancy: classification

According to WHO criteria, what haemoglobin level defines moderate anaemia in pregnancy?

- A. Hb < 11.0 g/dL
- B. Hb 7.0–9.9 g/dL
- C. Hb 10.0–10.9 g/dL
- D. Hb < 7.0 g/dL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Hb 7.0–9.9 g/dL** — WHO classification of anaemia in pregnancy: mild = Hb 10.0–10.9 g/dL; moderate = Hb 7.0–9.9 g/dL; severe = Hb < 7.0 g/dL. Normal lower limit in pregnancy is 11.0 g/dL (first/third trimester) or 10.5 g/dL (second trimester).

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Rhesus Isoimmunisation

### Q380 — Anti-D prophylaxis timing

An Rh-negative primigravida at 34 weeks with an Rh-positive partner is given anti-D immunoglobulin. What is the standard dose and timing for routine antenatal anti-D prophylaxis (RAADP) in the UK/India protocol?

- A. 500 IU at 28 weeks only
- B. 500 IU at 28 weeks AND 34 weeks
- C. 1500 IU as a single dose at 28 weeks
- D. 300 mcg (1500 IU) at 28 weeks and 34 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 1500 IU as a single dose at 28 weeks** — The NICE/RCOG single-dose RAADP protocol uses 1500 IU (300 mcg) at 28 weeks; the two-dose protocol uses 500 IU at 28 and 34 weeks. Both are acceptable; single-dose is now preferred in many centres for compliance.

</details>

---
### Q381 — Kleihauer-Betke test

A Kleihauer-Betke (acid elution) test is performed after delivery in an Rh-negative mother. What does this test measure?

- A. Maternal antibody titre against fetal red cells
- B. Volume of fetal red cells in the maternal circulation (fetomaternal haemorrhage)
- C. Fetal blood group
- D. Presence of anti-D antibodies in fetal serum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Volume of fetal red cells in the maternal circulation (fetomaternal haemorrhage)** — The Kleihauer-Betke test detects fetal (HbF-containing) red cells in the maternal blood by acid elution; it quantifies the extent of fetomaternal haemorrhage to determine if additional anti-D immunoglobulin is needed beyond the standard 500 IU dose.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Diabetes and Cardiac Disease in Pregnancy

### Q382 — GDM screening: DIPSI test

A 26-year-old pregnant woman undergoes the DIPSI (Diabetes in Pregnancy Study Group India) test at 24–28 weeks. She is given a 75 g glucose load without any prior fasting. A 2-hour plasma glucose of ≥ ________ mg/dL diagnoses GDM by DIPSI criteria.

- A. 120 mg/dL
- B. 140 mg/dL
- C. 153 mg/dL
- D. 200 mg/dL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 140 mg/dL** — DIPSI criteria: 2-hour plasma glucose ≥ 140 mg/dL after a 75 g non-fasting glucose load = GDM. This single-step test is recommended by MoHFW India for universal GDM screening due to its simplicity in resource-limited settings.

</details>

---
### Q383 — Cardiac disease in pregnancy: absolute contraindication

Which cardiac condition is an ABSOLUTE contraindication to pregnancy due to > 50% maternal mortality?

- A. Mitral stenosis (NYHA class II)
- B. Eisenmenger's syndrome (pulmonary hypertension with right-to-left shunt)
- C. Mechanical heart valve on warfarin
- D. Peripartum cardiomyopathy in previous pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Eisenmenger's syndrome (pulmonary hypertension with right-to-left shunt)** — Eisenmenger's syndrome carries > 50% maternal mortality in pregnancy (systemic vasodilation of pregnancy worsens right-to-left shunting, causing refractory hypoxaemia); it is an absolute contraindication to pregnancy, and termination should be recommended.

</details>

---
### Q384 — LMWH in pregnancy

A pregnant woman at 12 weeks with a previous deep vein thrombosis requires thromboprophylaxis. She is started on low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH). What is the advantage of LMWH over unfractionated heparin (UFH) in pregnancy?

- A. LMWH crosses the placenta and provides fetal anticoagulation
- B. LMWH does not cross the placenta and has more predictable pharmacokinetics
- C. LMWH can be used in heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT)
- D. LMWH has a shorter half-life allowing rapid reversal before delivery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. LMWH does not cross the placenta and has more predictable pharmacokinetics** — LMWH is preferred over UFH in pregnancy because it does not cross the placenta (safe for fetus), has predictable dosing requiring less monitoring, lower risk of heparin-induced osteoporosis, and lower risk of HIT compared to UFH.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Menopause and HRT

### Q385 — Menopause: vasomotor symptoms

A 51-year-old woman presents with frequent hot flushes and night sweats for 8 months following her last menstrual period. FSH is 60 IU/L. She has no contraindications. What is the MOST effective treatment for her vasomotor symptoms?

- A. Low-dose SSRIs (venlafaxine)
- B. Tibolone
- C. Combined oestrogen-progestogen HRT (systemic)
- D. Black cohosh supplements

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Combined oestrogen-progestogen HRT (systemic)** — Systemic HRT (oestrogen + progestogen for women with a uterus) is the most effective treatment for menopausal vasomotor symptoms; all other options (SSRIs, tibolone, phytoestrogens) are second-line alternatives for women who cannot take HRT.

</details>

---
### Q386 — HRT and endometrial protection

A 53-year-old woman with an intact uterus requires HRT for severe menopausal symptoms. Why must progestogen be added to oestrogen therapy?

- A. To prevent cardiovascular events
- B. To protect against oestrogen-induced endometrial hyperplasia and cancer
- C. To prevent breast cancer from oestrogen
- D. To improve mood and libido

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. To protect against oestrogen-induced endometrial hyperplasia and cancer** — Unopposed oestrogen stimulates endometrial proliferation, increasing endometrial hyperplasia and carcinoma risk; progestogen (continuous or cyclical) is mandatory in women with an intact uterus. Women after hysterectomy can take oestrogen-only HRT.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — AUB / DUB

### Q387 — PALM-COEIN classification

According to the FIGO PALM-COEIN classification of abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB), which of the following is a STRUCTURAL cause?

- A. Coagulopathy
- B. Ovulatory dysfunction
- C. Adenomyosis
- D. Endometrial (primary endometrial disorder)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Adenomyosis** — PALM (structural): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia; COEIN (non-structural): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified. Adenomyosis is "A" in PALM = structural.

</details>

---
### Q388 — AUB-O (ovulatory dysfunction)

A 35-year-old woman presents with irregular, heavy, unpredictable periods. She is obese with BMI 34. Pelvic ultrasound is normal, endometrial biopsy shows simple hyperplasia without atypia, and serum progesterone in luteal phase is < 3 ng/mL. The underlying mechanism of her AUB is most likely:

- A. AUB-L (leiomyoma)
- B. AUB-O (anovulatory cycles with unopposed oestrogen)
- C. AUB-C (coagulopathy)
- D. AUB-P (endometrial polyp)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. AUB-O (anovulatory cycles with unopposed oestrogen)** — Obesity causes peripheral aromatisation of androgens to oestrone, driving unopposed oestrogen stimulation of the endometrium without progesterone opposition (low luteal progesterone confirms anovulation), leading to irregular, heavy bleeding and endometrial hyperplasia.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Fistulae and Asherman's Syndrome

### Q389 — Vesicovaginal fistula (VVF)

A 26-year-old woman from rural India presents 7 days after an obstructed labour with continuous urinary incontinence. Examination shows a fistulous opening on the anterior vaginal wall. What is the MOST COMMON cause of VVF globally vs. in developed countries?

- A. Globally: gynaecological surgery; Developed: obstetric trauma
- B. Globally: obstetric trauma (prolonged obstructed labour); Developed: gynaecological surgery
- C. Globally: radiation therapy; Developed: obstetric trauma
- D. Globally: pelvic inflammatory disease; Developed: Crohn's disease

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Globally: obstetric trauma (prolonged obstructed labour); Developed: gynaecological surgery** — In developing countries, prolonged obstructed labour causing pressure necrosis accounts for > 90% of VVF; in developed countries, total abdominal hysterectomy (TAH) and other pelvic surgeries are the most common cause.

</details>

---
### Q390 — Asherman's syndrome

A 28-year-old woman with secondary amenorrhoea following suction evacuation for missed abortion undergoes hysteroscopy, which reveals intrauterine synechiae obliterating the cavity. What is the DEFINITIVE treatment?

- A. Combined oral contraceptive pill for 3 months
- B. GnRH agonist followed by add-back therapy
- C. Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis followed by high-dose oestrogen and intrauterine device/balloon
- D. IVF with donor embryos

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Hysteroscopic adhesiolysis followed by high-dose oestrogen and intrauterine device/balloon** — Treatment of Asherman's syndrome is hysteroscopic division of adhesions; post-operative high-dose oestrogen (± intrauterine device or Foley catheter balloon) prevents re-formation of adhesions and promotes endometrial regeneration.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Chromosomal and Developmental Disorders

### Q391 — Turner syndrome

A 16-year-old girl presents with primary amenorrhoea, short stature, and webbed neck. Karyotype is 45,X0. Which investigation would show streak gonads on imaging?

- A. MRI of pituitary
- B. Pelvic ultrasound
- C. Laparoscopy
- D. CT scan of abdomen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Pelvic ultrasound** — Pelvic ultrasound in Turner syndrome typically shows streak ovaries (fibrous bands replacing gonads), an infantile uterus, and absent follicles; serum FSH and LH are markedly elevated (hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism). Laparoscopy is definitive but ultrasound is first-line imaging.

</details>

---
### Q392 — Rokitansky (MRKH) syndrome

A 17-year-old presents with primary amenorrhoea, normal secondary sexual characteristics, and a blind vaginal pouch of 2 cm. Ultrasound shows absent uterus with bilateral normal ovaries. Karyotype is 46,XX. This is:

- A. Complete androgen insensitivity syndrome (CAIS)
- B. Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser (MRKH) syndrome
- C. Swyer syndrome (46,XY pure gonadal dysgenesis)
- D. Imperforate hymen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser (MRKH) syndrome** — MRKH: absent uterus + upper vagina (Müllerian aplasia), 46,XX karyotype, normal ovaries, normal female secondary sexual characteristics. Distinguished from CAIS by karyotype (46,XX vs 46,XY) and from imperforate hymen by absent uterus.

</details>

---
### Q393 — Androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS)

A phenotypically female 18-year-old presents with primary amenorrhoea and absence of pubic/axillary hair. Karyotype is 46,XY. Testes are found in the inguinal canals. What is the MOST IMPORTANT long-term management concern?

- A. Cyclical oestrogen-progestogen HRT to induce menstruation
- B. Gonadectomy to prevent malignant transformation of intra-abdominal/inguinal testes
- C. Testosterone supplementation to masculinise
- D. Clomiphene to stimulate gonadotrophin release

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Gonadectomy to prevent malignant transformation of intra-abdominal/inguinal testes** — In complete AIS (46,XY), the testes have a 2–5% lifetime risk of malignant transformation (gonadoblastoma, dysgerminoma); gonadectomy is recommended after puberty (allowing puberty to complete with endogenous oestrogen from testosterone aromatisation), followed by oestrogen-only HRT.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Benign Conditions

### Q394 — Primary dysmenorrhoea

A 19-year-old nulliparous woman presents with severe, crampy lower abdominal pain starting 2–4 hours before menses and lasting 48–72 hours, relieved by NSAIDs. Pelvic examination is normal. The PATHOPHYSIOLOGICAL mechanism is:

- A. Elevated serum oestradiol causing myometrial spasm
- B. Excess prostaglandin F2α causing myometrial ischaemia
- C. Retrograde menstruation causing peritoneal inflammation
- D. Elevated prolactin causing luteal phase deficiency

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Excess prostaglandin F2α causing myometrial ischaemia** — Primary dysmenorrhoea is caused by excessive prostaglandin F2α (PGF2α) production in the secretory endometrium, causing intense myometrial contractions → uterine ischaemia and pain; NSAIDs (COX inhibitors) reduce PGF2α synthesis and are first-line treatment.

</details>

---
### Q395 — Lichen sclerosus

A 60-year-old post-menopausal woman presents with severe vulval pruritus and white, parchment-like atrophic skin of the vulva in a "figure-of-8" distribution around the vulva and perianal area. Biopsy shows epidermal thinning and homogenised dermis. What is the TREATMENT OF CHOICE?

- A. Topical oestrogen cream
- B. High-potency topical corticosteroid (clobetasol propionate 0.05%)
- C. Topical antifungal (clotrimazole)
- D. Vulvectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. High-potency topical corticosteroid (clobetasol propionate 0.05%)** — Lichen sclerosus is treated with ultra-potent topical corticosteroids (clobetasol propionate); topical oestrogen is ineffective as it is NOT an oestrogenic condition. Long-term follow-up is needed as LS carries ~5% risk of squamous cell carcinoma of the vulva.

</details>

---
### Q396 — Bartholin's cyst management

A 32-year-old woman presents with a painless, 3 cm cystic swelling at the 5 o'clock position of the posterior vaginal introitus. It has been present for 6 months. No signs of infection. What is the PREFERRED definitive surgical management?

- A. Incision and drainage (I&D)
- B. Marsupialization
- C. Excision of the Bartholin's gland
- D. Word catheter insertion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Marsupialization** — Marsupialization (surgical creation of a new duct opening to allow drainage) is the preferred definitive treatment for recurrent or large Bartholin's cysts; I&D alone has a high recurrence rate; Word catheter is good for acute abscesses; excision is reserved for older women (risk of haematoma, dyspareunia).

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Infection and Inflammation

### Q397 — Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome

A 24-year-old sexually active woman presents with right upper quadrant pain, fever, and cervical motion tenderness on pelvic examination. Liver function tests are mildly elevated. Laparoscopy shows "violin string" adhesions between the liver capsule and anterior abdominal wall. This is:

- A. Acute cholecystitis
- B. Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (perihepatitis complicating PID)
- C. Hepatic abscess
- D. Budd-Chiari syndrome

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (perihepatitis complicating PID)** — Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome is perihepatitis caused by spread of Neisseria gonorrhoeae or Chlamydia trachomatis from the pelvis to the liver capsule; characteristic "violin string" adhesions on laparoscopy with RUQ pain + signs of PID. Treatment is same as for PID.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Urinary Incontinence

### Q398 — Stress urinary incontinence: mechanism

A 45-year-old multiparous woman leaks urine on coughing, sneezing, and exercise. She denies urgency or frequency. Urodynamics shows involuntary urine loss with increased intra-abdominal pressure without detrusor contraction. The pathophysiological mechanism is:

- A. Detrusor overactivity
- B. Inadequate urethral closure mechanism (urethral sphincter deficiency / hypermobility)
- C. Vesicovaginal fistula
- D. Overflow incontinence from detrusor underactivity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Inadequate urethral closure mechanism (urethral sphincter deficiency / hypermobility)** — Stress urinary incontinence results from failure of the urethral closure pressure to exceed bladder pressure during physical stress; caused by pelvic floor weakness, urethral hypermobility, or intrinsic sphincter deficiency (ISD) — not detrusor overactivity (which causes urge incontinence).

</details>

---
### Q399 — Urgency incontinence: first-line treatment

A 55-year-old woman complains of sudden, compelling urge to void followed by involuntary urine leakage. She voids 12 times per day and twice at night. Urodynamics confirms detrusor overactivity. What is the FIRST-LINE pharmacological treatment?

- A. Duloxetine (SNRI)
- B. Oxybutynin (anticholinergic)
- C. Mirabegron (beta-3 adrenergic agonist)
- D. Topical oestrogen vaginal cream

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Oxybutynin (anticholinergic)** — Anticholinergics (oxybutynin, solifenacin, tolterodine) are first-line pharmacological treatment for overactive bladder / detrusor overactivity; they reduce detrusor contractions by blocking muscarinic receptors. Mirabegron is an alternative with fewer anticholinergic side effects.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Reproductive Medicine

### Q400 — IVF: ovarian hyperstimulation (OHSS)

A 29-year-old woman with PCOS undergoing IVF develops severe abdominal distension, nausea, and shortness of breath 5 days after oocyte retrieval. Ultrasound shows bilateral enlarged ovaries (each > 10 cm) with massive ascites. Haematocrit is 52%. This complication is:

- A. Ruptured ovarian cyst
- B. Severe ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome (OHSS)
- C. Ovarian torsion
- D. Pelvic inflammatory disease post-retrieval

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Severe ovarian hyperstimulation syndrome (OHSS)** — Severe OHSS: ovaries > 10 cm, massive ascites, haemoconcentration (Hct > 45%), electrolyte imbalances, risk of VTE; most common in PCOS women. Triggered by hCG (exogenous or endogenous from early pregnancy); using GnRH agonist trigger instead of hCG reduces risk.

</details>

---
### Q401 — Precocious puberty: GnRH-dependent vs independent

An 8-year-old girl presents with breast development (Tanner stage 3), pubic hair, and accelerated height velocity. GnRH stimulation test shows LH/FSH surge (LH peak > 5 IU/L), confirming the diagnosis is GnRH-dependent (central) precocious puberty. What is the TREATMENT OF CHOICE?

- A. Oral combined contraceptive pill
- B. GnRH agonist (e.g., leuprolide acetate depot)
- C. Medroxyprogesterone acetate
- D. Aromatase inhibitor (letrozole)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. GnRH agonist (e.g., leuprolide acetate depot)** — GnRH agonists (leuprolide, triptorelin) given continuously cause pituitary desensitisation and downregulation of the HPG axis, halting puberty progression and preserving adult height; they are the standard of care for central (GnRH-dependent) precocious puberty.

</details>

---
### Q402 — Precocious puberty: GnRH-independent cause

A 7-year-old girl has breast development and vaginal bleeding but a GnRH stimulation test shows NO LH/FSH surge (LH remains suppressed). Ultrasound shows a 6 cm unilateral ovarian cyst. The MOST LIKELY cause is:

- A. Constitutional (idiopathic) central precocious puberty
- B. Autonomous follicular cyst (McCune-Albright syndrome or isolated)
- C. Congenital adrenal hyperplasia
- D. Craniopharyngioma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Autonomous follicular cyst (McCune-Albright syndrome or isolated)** — GnRH-independent (peripheral) precocious puberty with a unilateral ovarian cyst and suppressed gonadotrophins indicates autonomous oestrogen secretion; isolated follicular cysts (often resolve spontaneously) or McCune-Albright syndrome (GNAS1 mutation with café-au-lait spots and polyostotic fibrous dysplasia) are the key causes.

</details>

---

## Obstetrics — Antepartum Haemorrhage

### Q403 — APH: Vasa praevia distinction (clinical scenario)

A primigravida at 38 weeks presents with sudden onset painless bright red vaginal bleeding immediately after spontaneous rupture of membranes. Fetal bradycardia (FHR 80 bpm) develops within minutes. What diagnosis must be IMMEDIATELY excluded?

- A. Placenta praevia
- B. Placental abruption
- C. Vasa praevia with fetal vessel rupture
- D. Cervical ectropion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Vasa praevia with fetal vessel rupture** — The hallmark of vasa praevia rupture is painless bright red bleeding at the time of membrane rupture + immediate fetal distress; bleeding is FETAL blood (not maternal), hence rapid fetal exsanguination and death without immediate caesarean. APH in vasa praevia = obstetric emergency.

</details>

---
### Q404 — Antepartum haemorrhage: placental abruption severity

A 32-year-old multigravida at 36 weeks presents with sudden severe abdominal pain and a "woody hard" uterus. She has no visible vaginal bleeding. The fetus is in distress. What type of placental abruption is this?

- A. Type I (revealed/mild) — partial separation with external haemorrhage
- B. Type II (concealed/severe) — complete separation with retroplacental haematoma
- C. Type III — Mixed (partially revealed)
- D. Marginal sinus rupture

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Type II (concealed/severe) — complete separation with retroplacental haematoma** — Concealed abruption: blood trapped behind the placenta → no visible PV bleeding, severe abdominal pain, board-like uterus, fetal distress, and often DIC; it is more dangerous than revealed abruption because blood loss is underestimated.

</details>

---

## Gynaecology — Pelvic Floor Anatomy

### Q405 — Levator ani anatomy

Which muscles form the levator ani (the main component of the pelvic diaphragm)?

- A. Obturator internus, piriformis, coccygeus
- B. Pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus, ischiococcygeus (coccygeus)
- C. Puborectalis, bulbospongiosus, superficial transverse perinei
- D. External sphincter, deep transverse perinei, ischiocavernosus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus, ischiococcygeus (coccygeus)** — The levator ani is composed of pubococcygeus (including puborectalis) and iliococcygeus; together with coccygeus (ischiococcygeus) they form the pelvic diaphragm. Damage during childbirth predisposes to pelvic organ prolapse and stress incontinence.

</details>

---
### Q406 — Stress incontinence: surgical treatment

A 50-year-old woman with stress urinary incontinence has failed pelvic floor physiotherapy for 6 months. Urodynamics confirms urethral hypermobility. Which surgery has the HIGHEST long-term cure rate?

- A. Anterior colporrhaphy (anterior repair)
- B. Burch colposuspension
- C. Mid-urethral tape (TVT or TOT)
- D. Periurethral collagen injection

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Mid-urethral tape (TVT or TOT)** — Mid-urethral slings (tension-free vaginal tape [TVT] or transobturator tape [TOT]) have become the gold-standard surgical treatment for stress incontinence with > 85% cure rates and are preferred over Burch colposuspension (similar efficacy but more invasive) and anterior repair (lower cure rate).

</details>

---
### Q407 — DUB: tranexamic acid

A 38-year-old woman with heavy regular periods (changing pads every 2 hours) and normal pelvic ultrasound and endometrial biopsy (AUB-E) does not wish to use hormonal treatment. What is the MOST APPROPRIATE non-hormonal first-line treatment?

- A. Norethisterone 5 mg three times daily cyclically
- B. Tranexamic acid 1 g three times daily during menstruation
- C. Danazol 200 mg daily
- D. GnRH agonist monthly injection

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Tranexamic acid 1 g three times daily during menstruation** — Tranexamic acid (antifibrinolytic) reduces menstrual blood loss by 40–50% and is the first-line non-hormonal pharmacological treatment for heavy menstrual bleeding (HMB); NSAIDs (mefenamic acid) are an alternative non-hormonal option. Hormonal options (LNG-IUS, OCPs) are preferred if contraception is also required.

</details>

---

## Pharmacology & Physiology

### Q408 — Drugs NOT crossing placenta

Which of the following drugs does NOT cross the placental barrier?

- A. Heparin
- B. Morphine
- C. Naloxone
- D. Warfarin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Heparin** — Heparin is a large, highly charged molecule that cannot cross the placenta; warfarin, morphine, and naloxone all cross freely. Heparin is therefore preferred as anticoagulant in pregnancy.

</details>

---
### Q409 — HRT: NOT indicated for CAD prevention

HRT is NOT recommended for which of the following indications?

- A. Hot flushes and night sweats
- B. Prevention of coronary artery disease
- C. Osteoporosis prevention
- D. Vaginal atrophy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Prevention of coronary artery disease** — The WHI trial showed HRT (combined oestrogen-progestogen) does not protect against CAD; it may increase cardiovascular risk in older postmenopausal women. HRT relieves vasomotor symptoms, prevents bone loss, and treats vaginal atrophy.

</details>

---
### Q410 — Latent phase of labour: false statement

Which of the following is NOT true about the latent phase of labour?

- A. Starts with onset of regular uterine contractions
- B. Begins at the end of the active phase
- C. Ends at 6 cm cervical dilatation (ACOG 2014)
- D. Patient may present with mild crampy pain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Begins at the end of the active phase** — The latent phase PRECEDES the active phase; it begins with onset of regular uterine contractions and ends at 6 cm dilatation. False labour may mimic the latent phase.

</details>

---
### Q411 — Implantation timing

How many days after ovulation does implantation of the blastocyst occur?

- A. 3–5 days
- B. 7–9 days
- C. 10–12 days
- D. 13–15 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 7–9 days** — Implantation occurs approximately 7–9 days after ovulation (day 20–22 of a 28-day cycle); the blastocyst hatches from the zona pellucida around day 5–6 before implanting into the secretory endometrium.

</details>

---
### Q412 — Sheehan's syndrome

A 35-year-old multiparous woman has 12 months of amenorrhoea, failure of lactation after her second delivery, and an empty sella on skull imaging. The diagnosis is:

- A. Menopause
- B. Pituitary adenoma
- C. Sheehan's syndrome
- D. Intraductal papilloma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Sheehan's syndrome** — Postpartum pituitary necrosis (Sheehan's) follows massive obstetric haemorrhage causing pituitary ischaemia; classic triad: agalactia, secondary amenorrhoea, empty sella. Anterior pituitary hormones are deficient (GH, FSH/LH, TSH, ACTH, prolactin).

</details>

---
### Q413 — Displaced IUCD management

A woman with a copper IUD inserted 2 years ago has absent threads on speculum examination; pelvic ultrasound shows the device partially displaced into the abdominal cavity. Management:

- A. Hysteroscopy
- B. No removal needed
- C. IUCD hook retrieval
- D. Laparoscopy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Laparoscopy** — A displaced IUD in the abdominal cavity must be removed laparoscopically to prevent bowel adhesions, perforation, and other visceral injury.

</details>

---
### Q414 — Cervical cancer FIGO staging: investigation excluded

All of the following investigations are used in FIGO staging of carcinoma cervix (traditional pre-2018 system) EXCEPT:

- A. CT scan
- B. Intravenous pyelography
- C. Cystoscopy
- D. Proctoscopy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. CT scan** — Traditional FIGO cervical cancer staging (pre-2018 revision) was clinical only; permitted investigations included IVP, cystoscopy, proctoscopy, chest X-ray, and barium enema. CT/MRI/PET were not permitted for staging (though used for treatment planning).

</details>

---
### Q415 — DUB: not a postmenopausal diagnosis

Dysfunctional uterine bleeding (DUB) is seen in all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Adolescence
- B. Following childbirth
- C. Premenopausal period
- D. Postmenopausal period

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Postmenopausal period** — DUB is by definition anovulatory bleeding in a woman of reproductive age; it does not occur after menopause. Postmenopausal bleeding always requires investigation to exclude endometrial carcinoma.

</details>

---
### Q416 — Earliest symptom of carcinoma cervix

The EARLIEST symptom of carcinoma cervix is:

- A. Irregular vaginal bleeding
- B. Post-coital bleeding
- C. Foul-smelling vaginal discharge
- D. Pelvic pain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Irregular vaginal bleeding** — The earliest symptom is irregular, painless vaginal bleeding; post-coital bleeding is characteristic but appears as the lesion becomes more advanced. Pain is a late symptom.

</details>

---
### Q417 — Androgen exposure in female fetus

Excess androgen exposure of a female fetus in early embryogenesis leads to virilisation of:

- A. Müllerian ducts
- B. Ovary
- C. Urogenital sinus
- D. Mesonephric ducts

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Urogenital sinus** — Excess androgens cause virilisation of the urogenital sinus (labioscrotal fusion, clitoral hypertrophy, posterior fusion) while Müllerian-derived internal genitalia (uterus, tubes, upper vagina) develop normally; seen in congenital adrenal hyperplasia (21α-hydroxylase deficiency).

</details>

---
### Q418 — Ideal contraceptive for lactating mother

The ideal contraceptive for a woman who is exclusively breastfeeding is:

- A. Barrier method alone
- B. Combined oral contraceptive pill
- C. Lactational amenorrhoea alone
- D. Progestogen-only pill

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Progestogen-only pill** — Progestogen-only pills (mini-pills) do not suppress prolactin or milk production, unlike combined OCPs (oestrogen suppresses lactation). LAM alone is unreliable beyond 6 months or when breastfeeding is not exclusive.

</details>

---
### Q419 — CIN III on PAP smear: next step

A PAP smear in a 45-year-old woman shows CIN grade III (HSIL). The BEST next logical procedure is:

- A. Cold-knife conisation
- B. Colposcopy + directed biopsy
- C. Hysterectomy
- D. HPV DNA typing

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Colposcopy + directed biopsy** — Abnormal cytology must be confirmed histologically before treatment. Colposcopy identifies the lesion; directed biopsy provides tissue diagnosis for histological grading before proceeding to LLETZ/cone/hysterectomy.

</details>

---
### Q420 — Metropathia haemorrhagica

A period of amenorrhoea followed by sudden massive irregular bleeding in a premenopausal woman suggests:

- A. Irregular ripening
- B. Irregular shedding
- C. Metropathia haemorrhagica
- D. Normal menstruation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Metropathia haemorrhagica** — Prolonged oestrogenic stimulation without progesterone (anovulation) leads to cystic glandular hyperplasia; when the oestrogen level falls, the thickened hyperplastic endometrium sheds irregularly causing heavy breakthrough bleeding after amenorrhoea.

</details>

---
### Q421 — Imaging for uterine fibroids: false statement

Regarding imaging of uterine fibroids, which statement is FALSE?

- A. Ultrasound is ideal to confirm the diagnosis
- B. Saline infusion sonography (SIS) better detects submucous fibroids
- C. MRI is superior to ultrasound for identifying exact fibroid location
- D. CT scanning is an adequate alternative to MRI

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. CT scanning is an adequate alternative to MRI** — CT has poor soft-tissue contrast and cannot delineate fibroid location, vascularity, and relationship to endometrium as well as MRI; MRI is the gold standard for fibroid mapping pre-myomectomy or UAE.

</details>

---
### Q422 — LNG-IUS (Mirena): NOT indicated for

Levonorgestrel-releasing IUD (LNG-IUS) is helpful in all of the following EXCEPT:

- A. Menorrhagia
- B. Dysmenorrhoea
- C. Endometriosis
- D. Pelvic inflammatory disease

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Pelvic inflammatory disease** — LNG-IUS helps in menorrhagia, dysmenorrhoea, and endometriosis. It is contraindicated in active PID and does not treat or prevent PID; the local progestogenic environment reduces ascent risk but does not confer protection against gonorrhoea/chlamydia.

</details>

---
### Q423 — Corpus cancer syndrome: NOT a feature

Which of the following is NOT seen in Corpus Cancer syndrome (endometrial cancer risk triad)?

- A. Diabetes mellitus
- B. Hypertension
- C. Obesity
- D. Multiparity

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Multiparity** — The classic Corpus Cancer Syndrome triad is obesity + hypertension + diabetes (all associated with unopposed oestrogen from peripheral aromatisation). NULLIPARITY (not multiparity) is a risk factor for endometrial cancer.

</details>

---
### Q424 — Tubercular salpingitis: false statement

All of the following about tubercular salpingitis are correct EXCEPT:

- A. The abdominal ostium may be patent with eversion of fimbriae
- B. Early lesions may be confused with adenocarcinoma on histology
- C. Genital tuberculosis is always secondary, with tubes as primary genital site
- D. Salpingitis isthmica nodosa is exclusive to tuberculosis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Salpingitis isthmica nodosa is exclusive to tuberculosis** — SIN (diverticular outpouching of tubal epithelium into the muscularis) is not exclusive to TB; it also occurs with gonorrhoeal and other infections. All other statements are correct.

</details>

---
### Q425 — Amniotic fluid: maximum volume timing

Amniotic fluid volume is MAXIMUM at:

- A. 32–34 weeks
- B. 36–38 weeks
- C. 34–36 weeks
- D. 38–40 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 36–38 weeks** — Amniotic fluid volume peaks at approximately 36–38 weeks (~1,000 mL) then gradually decreases toward term (and post-term), providing the rationale for induction in post-term pregnancies with oligohydramnios.

</details>

---
### Q426 — Boari flap operation

"Boari flap" is a surgical technique used for:

- A. Renal pelvic repair
- B. Urinary diversion
- C. Bladder flap to bridge a ureteric defect
- D. Vesicovaginal fistula repair

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Bladder flap to bridge a ureteric defect** — The Boari flap uses a tubularised flap of bladder wall to bridge and reconstruct a distal ureteric defect, commonly used when the ureter is injured (usually right ureter) during gynaecological surgery.

</details>

---
### Q427 — Clue cells

"Clue cells" on vaginal wet mount are pathognomonic of:

- A. Bacterial vaginosis
- B. Vaginal candidiasis
- C. Chlamydial vaginitis
- D. Trichomoniasis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Bacterial vaginosis** — Clue cells are vaginal squamous epithelial cells studded with Gardnerella vaginalis (and anaerobes), giving a stippled appearance on wet mount; they are one of Amsel's four criteria for BV diagnosis.

</details>

---
### Q428 — Congenital cervical ectropion: reappearance

"Congenital" cervical erosion (ectropion) regresses after birth but may reappear at:

- A. One year of age
- B. Two years of age
- C. Five years of age
- D. Puberty

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Puberty** — Congenital cervical ectropion is present at birth due to maternal oestrogen; it regresses under declining oestrogen after birth but reappears at puberty when endogenous oestrogen stimulation causes eversion of the columnar epithelium.

</details>

---
### Q429 — Double set-up examination

"Double set-up examination" (vaginal examination prepared for immediate caesarean section) is specifically done in the diagnosis and management of:

- A. Placenta praevia
- B. Manual removal of placenta
- C. Twin pregnancy
- D. Bicornuate uterus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Placenta praevia** — Double set-up examination is performed when placenta praevia is suspected but unconfirmed and the patient needs delivery; it is done in the operating theatre with full preparation for immediate caesarean section in case brisk bleeding occurs on digital examination.

</details>

---
### Q430 — Eosinophilic index

The eosinophilic (karyopyknotic) index of lateral vaginal wall cells indicates:

- A. Effect of LH
- B. Oestrogenic effect
- C. Progesterone effect
- D. Prolactin effect

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Oestrogenic effect** — The eosinophilic index (% of superficial cells with eosinophilic cytoplasm + pyknotic nuclei) reflects oestrogenic stimulation of the vaginal epithelium; it peaks at ovulation and is used in vaginal cytology to assess hormonal status.

</details>

---

## Gynaecological Oncology — Ovarian

### Q431 — Dysgerminoma: secreted marker

Which product is secreted by dysgerminoma?

- A. Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP)
- B. Human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG)
- C. Placental alkaline phosphatase (PLAP)
- D. Serum inhibin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Placental alkaline phosphatase (PLAP)** — Pure dysgerminoma (ovarian counterpart of seminoma) secretes PLAP and LDH; it does NOT secrete AFP or hCG (those markers suggest yolk sac tumour or choriocarcinoma components). CA-125 is not specific.

</details>

---
### Q432 — Dysgerminoma: monitoring marker

In a confirmed case of dysgerminoma, which serum marker is monitored for treatment response and recurrence?

- A. Beta-hCG
- B. Alpha-fetoprotein
- C. Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)
- D. Serum inhibin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)** — LDH (especially isoenzyme 1) is the preferred serum marker for monitoring dysgerminoma; it reflects tumour burden and response to chemotherapy/radiotherapy.

</details>

---
### Q433 — Germ cell tumours of ovary: exception

All of the following are germ cell tumours of the ovary EXCEPT:

- A. Mesonephroid (clear cell) tumour
- B. Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid)
- C. Dysgerminoma
- D. Endodermal sinus tumour (yolk sac tumour)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Mesonephroid (clear cell) tumour** — Clear cell carcinoma is an EPITHELIAL (surface epithelial-stromal) tumour, NOT a germ cell tumour. Germ cell tumours arise from primordial germ cells: dysgerminoma, teratoma, yolk sac tumour, embryonal carcinoma, choriocarcinoma.

</details>

---
### Q434 — Endometrioid ovarian tumour: frequency

Which ovarian tumour accounts for approximately 20% of all ovarian cancers and often co-exists with ovarian endometriosis?

- A. Serous cystadenocarcinoma
- B. Mucinous cystadenocarcinoma
- C. Endometrioid carcinoma
- D. Brenner tumour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Endometrioid carcinoma** — Endometrioid ovarian carcinoma accounts for ~20% of ovarian cancers; it shows glandular differentiation resembling endometrial glands and co-exists with endometriosis in ~15% of cases; associated with PTEN and ARID1A mutations.

</details>

---
### Q435 — Pseudomyxoma peritonei origin

Pseudomyxoma peritonei (massive gelatinous peritoneal deposits) most commonly occurs as a complication of rupture of:

- A. Serous cystadenoma
- B. Mucinous cystadenoma / low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm
- C. Dysgerminoma
- D. Gonadoblastoma

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Mucinous cystadenoma / low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm** — Rupture of a mucinous ovarian or appendiceal neoplasm seeds the peritoneum with mucin-secreting epithelial cells; the appendix is now considered the primary site in most cases of true pseudomyxoma peritonei.

</details>

---
### Q436 — LLETZ: false statement

Which of the following is NOT true regarding Large Loop Excision of the Transformation Zone (LLETZ)?

- A. Uses low-voltage diathermy
- B. Applicable only to the cervix
- C. Loop size > 2 cm gives a better cone specimen
- D. Shorter procedure time than laser ablation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Loop size > 2 cm gives a better cone specimen** — FALSE: a loop size LESS than 2 cm gives a better, more precise cone specimen with less thermal artefact at the margins. LLETZ uses low-voltage diathermy, is cervix-specific, and is faster than laser ablation.

</details>

---
### Q437 — Endometrial hyperplasia: highest cancer risk

Which histological pattern of endometrial hyperplasia carries the HIGHEST risk of progression to endometrial carcinoma?

- A. Simple hyperplasia without atypia (~1%)
- B. Simple hyperplasia with atypia (~8%)
- C. Complex hyperplasia without atypia (~3%)
- D. Complex hyperplasia with atypia (~29%)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Complex hyperplasia with atypia (~29%)** — Atypia is the key determinant of malignant potential; complex atypical hyperplasia (EIN — Endometrial Intraepithelial Neoplasia) carries ~29% risk and is managed by hysterectomy in perimenopausal/older women or continuous progestin in young women desiring fertility.

</details>

---
### Q438 — Moschcowitz repair: indication

Moschcowitz procedure (purse-string obliteration of the pouch of Douglas) is performed for:

- A. Vault prolapse
- B. Enterocoele
- C. Chronic uterine inversion
- D. Adenomyosis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Enterocoele** — Moschcowitz procedure: serial concentric purse-string sutures obliterate the peritoneal cul-de-sac (pouch of Douglas) to repair or prevent enterocoele; care must be taken not to incorporate the ureter in the suture.

</details>

---
### Q439 — Savage syndrome

Which of the following is TRUE regarding Savage syndrome (resistant ovary syndrome)?

- A. There is a receptor defect to gonadotrophic hormones
- B. Patient has short stature
- C. Ovaries contain no follicles
- D. FSH level is normal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Receptor defect to gonadotrophic hormones** — Savage syndrome: ovaries contain follicles but are unresponsive to FSH/LH due to a gonadotrophin receptor defect → raised FSH/LH, normal height, normal-appearing ovaries with follicles but no ovulation. Distinguishes from premature ovarian failure (POF), which has depleted follicles.

</details>

---
### Q440 — PCOS: cyst formation mechanism

The multiple small cysts seen in PCOS on ultrasound are formed by:

- A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis
- B. Oocyte proliferation
- C. Formation of multiple corpora lutea
- D. Cystic degeneration of the ovarian cortex

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Failure of atretic follicles to undergo apoptosis** — Elevated androgens in PCOS inhibit follicular apoptosis (atresia arrest); multiple small follicles (2–9 mm) accumulate peripherally in the ovary in a "necklace" pattern without a dominant follicle developing or ovulation occurring.

</details>

---
### Q441 — Lactational amenorrhoea: mechanism

Lactational amenorrhoea is maintained primarily by:

- A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH pulsatility
- B. Prolactin-induced inhibition of FSH secretion
- C. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of GnRH
- D. Oxytocin-induced inhibition of FSH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Prolactin-induced inhibition of GnRH pulsatility** — Suckling → prolactin surge → inhibits hypothalamic GnRH pulse generator → reduced FSH/LH → anovulation. Prolactin also sensitises the hypothalamus to negative oestrogen feedback. LAM is reliable only with exclusive breastfeeding, amenorrhoea, and age < 6 months postpartum.

</details>

---
### Q442 — Viral hepatitis: highest maternal mortality

With which type of viral hepatitis is maternal mortality HIGHEST in pregnancy?

- A. Hepatitis A
- B. Hepatitis B
- C. Hepatitis C
- D. Hepatitis E

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Hepatitis E** — Maternal mortality with hepatitis E virus (HEV) in pregnancy is 15–25% (especially in the 3rd trimester); fulminant hepatic failure is far more common with HEV than other hepatitis viruses in pregnancy. HEV is waterborne (faeco-oral).

</details>

---
### Q443 — Recurrent abortion + prolonged APTT

A woman with three consecutive pregnancy losses is found to have an isolated prolonged APTT. The most likely associated condition is:

- A. Lupus anticoagulant (antiphospholipid syndrome)
- B. Disseminated intravascular coagulation
- C. von Willebrand disease
- D. Haemophilia A

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Lupus anticoagulant (antiphospholipid syndrome)** — APS classically causes recurrent pregnancy loss + thrombosis; lupus anticoagulant prolongs APTT in vitro (paradoxically pro-thrombotic in vivo due to phospholipid-binding). Treatment in pregnancy: low-dose aspirin + LMWH.

</details>

---
### Q444 — Shortest fetal head diameter

The shortest diameter of the fetal head is:

- A. Biparietal diameter (9.5 cm)
- B. Suboccipitofrontal diameter (10 cm)
- C. Occipitofrontal diameter (11.5 cm)
- D. Bitemporal diameter (8 cm)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Bitemporal diameter (8 cm)** — The bitemporal diameter (between the two temporal bones) is the shortest of all fetal head diameters. The suboccipitobregmatic (9.5 cm) is the presenting AP diameter of a well-flexed vertex.

</details>

---
### Q445 — Progestasert IUCD: daily hormone release

The quantity of progesterone released by the Progestasert IUCD per day is:

- A. 25 µg
- B. 45 µg
- C. 65 µg
- D. 85 µg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 65 µg** — Progestasert contains 38 mg progesterone in a silicone oil reservoir releasing 65 µg/day; valid for 1 year. Compare LNG-IUS (Mirena): releases 20 µg levonorgestrel/day, valid 5 years. Higher local progestogenic effect → thickened cervical mucus.

</details>

---
### Q446 — Emergency contraception: NOT a mechanism

Emergency contraception prevents pregnancy by all of the following mechanisms EXCEPT:

- A. Delaying or inhibiting ovulation
- B. Inhibiting fertilisation
- C. Preventing implantation of fertilised egg
- D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Interrupting an early established pregnancy** — Emergency contraception (levonorgestrel, ulipristal) prevents pregnancy; once implantation is complete, EC is ineffective. Interrupting an existing pregnancy constitutes termination, not contraception. LNG acts primarily by delaying/inhibiting ovulation.

</details>

---
### Q447 — Mifepristone (RU-486): true statement

Which of the following is TRUE regarding RU-486 (mifepristone)?

- A. It prevents fertilisation
- B. It has affinity for oestrogen receptors
